Fullness of the Kuznetsov–Polishchuk exceptional collection for the spinor tenfold
Riccardo Moschetti, Marco Rampazzo

Abstract
. Kuznetsov and Polishchuk provided a general algorithm to construct exceptional collections of maximal length for homogeneous varieties of type A , B , C , D A,B,C,D . We consider the case of the spinor tenfold and we prove that the corresponding collection is full, i.e. it generates the whole derived category of coherent sheaves. As a step of the proof, we construct some resolutions of homogeneous vector bundles which might be of independent interest.



1.
Introduction

The derived category of coherent sheaves has been a subject of study in algebraic geometry for several decades: proven to be a powerful invariant for Fano and general type varieties by means of the reconstruction theorem
[
BO01
]
and for
K3
surfaces due to the derived Torelli theorem
[
Orl03
,
Huy09
]
, in general the interplay of the derived category with other invariants – such as the Hodge structure and the class in the Grothendieck ring of varieties – has yet to be fully understood.
While these categories are usually complicated to describe, a common approach to understanding their structure is by providing a so-called
full exceptional collection
, which is, roughly speaking, a finite list of objects which generate the whole category and satisfy simple cohomological conditions. While it is well-known that some classes of varieties cannot admit full exceptional collection, it is very much expected that rational homogeneous varieties do.

When it comes to the problem of constructing full exceptional collections for rational homogeneous varieties, the techniques developed in
[
Beĭ78
]
for projective spaces have been successfully generalized to Grassmannians and smooth quadrics in
[
Kap85
]
.
However, a general approach is still missing: only partial answers have been given in the case of classical and exceptional Grassmannians, mostly using specific, ad-hoc techniques.
Up to products, every rational homogeneous variety can be obtained as a quotient
G/P^{i}
where
P^{i}\subset G
is the maximal parabolic subgroup corresponding to the
i
-th simple root (more on this notation in Section
2
). Nowadays, to the best of the authors’ knowledge, full exceptional collections have been found for the following classes for every positive integer
n
, denoting the number of nodes in the corresponding Dynkin diagram:

•

G=A_{n}
,
P=P^{k}
for any
k

[
Beĭ78
,
Kap85
]
,

•

G=B_{n}
,
P=P^{1}
(
[
Kap85
]
) or
P=P^{2}
(
[
Kuz08
]
),

•

G=C_{n}
,
P=P^{1}
(
[
Beĭ78
,
Kap85
]
),
P=P^{2}
(
[
Kuz08
]
) or
P=P^{n}
(
[
Fon20
]
),

•

G=D_{n}
,
P=P^{1}
(
[
Kap85
]
) or
P=P^{2}
(
[
KS21
]
),

Moreover, the following sporadic cases are also known to admit a full exceptional collection:

•

G=B_{3}
,
P=P^{3}
(
[
Kap85
]
) and
G=B_{4}
,
P=P^{4}
(
[
Kuz06
]
,
[
Kuz08
]
),

•

G=C_{4}
,
P=P^{3}
(
[
Gus20
]
) and
G=C_{5}
,
P=P^{3}
(
[
Nov20
]
),

•

G=D_{i}
,
P=P^{i}
or
P=P^{i-1}
for
i=4
(
[
Kap85
]
) and
i=5
(
[
Kuz06
]
,
[
Kuz08
]
),

•

G=E_{6}
,
P=P^{1}
or
P=P^{6}
(
[
FM15
]
),

•

G=F_{4}
,
P=P^{1}
(
[
Smi22
]
) or
P=P^{4}
(
[
BKS23
]
),

•

G=G_{2}
,
P=P^{1}
(
[
Kap85
]
) or
P=P^{2}
(
[
Kuz06
]
).

A crucial step towards a general answer to this problem is provided by Kuznetsov and Polishchuk in
[
KP16
]
: there, exceptional collections of maximal length are given for all Grassmannians of type
A,B,C,D
, where the maximal length of an exceptional collection for
D^{b}(X)
with
X
smooth projective is
\sum_{p}h^{(p,p)}(X)
(see
[
Kuz16
, Corollary 2.16]
). In the following, we will refer to them as KP collections.
However, fullness is still conjectural: in other words, it is not known whether the KP collection for a given
G/P
generates the whole
D^{b}(G/P)
. In this paper, we perform the construction of Kuznetsov and Polishchuk explicitly for the spinor tenfold (i.e. a connected component of the Grassmannian of isotropic five spaces in a ten dimensional vector space) obtaining:

(1.1)

\begin{split}\langle&{\mathcal{O}},{\mathcal{O}}(1),{\mathcal{O}}(2),{\mathcal{U}}^{\vee}(2),\Sym^{2}{\mathcal{U}}^{\vee}(2),{\mathcal{O}}(3),{\mathcal{U}}^{\vee}(3),\Sym^{2}{\mathcal{U}}^{\vee}(3),\\
&\hskip 40.0pt{\mathcal{O}}(4),{\mathcal{U}}^{\vee}(4),\widehat{T}_{X}(5),{\mathcal{O}}(5),{\mathcal{U}}^{\vee}(5),\widehat{T}_{X}(6),{\mathcal{O}}(6),{\mathcal{O}}(7)\rangle\end{split}

where
{\mathcal{U}}^{\vee}
is the dual of the pullback of the tautological bundle of
G(5,10)
and
\widehat{T}_{X}
is the affine tangent bundle (see Equation
2.9
). Then, as a main result, we have the following fact:

Theorem 1.1
.

(Corollary
3.9
)

The exceptional collection of Equation
1.1
is full.

The proof is given by finding a sequence of mutations (see Section
3.1
) which relate such collection to the one found by Kuznetsov in
[
Kuz06
, Section 6.2]
. Since the latter is full, we conclude that also the KP collection is (Proposition
3.8
).

Plan of the paper

In Section
2
we give a brief introduction to the spinor tenfold and some vector bundles over such variety. Then, we establish resolutions of some homogeneous vector bundles (Lemma
2.4
and Lemma
2.5
). In Section
3
we perform the construction of the KP collection, and we prove its fullness in Section
3.3
. Finally, all the technical details of the cohomology computations based on the Borel–Bott–Weil theorem are carried out in Appendix
A
.

1.1.
Notations and conventions

We shall work over the field of complex numbers.

We will denote the cohomology of vector bundles as a direct sum of shifted cohomology spaces, and we will use the same notation for Ext spaces. For example,
\operatorname{Ext}_{{\mathbb{P}}^{n}}^{\bullet}({\mathcal{O}},\Omega_{{\mathbb{P}}^{n}}^{1})\simeq{\mathbb{C}}[-1]
.

2.
Vector bundles on the spinor tenfold

2.1.
The spinor tenfold and its properties

Fix a vector space
V_{n}
of dimension
n
. Let us call
G(k,V_{n})
the Grassmannian parametrizing
k
-dimensional subspaces of
V_{n}
. It is a smooth projective variety of dimension
k(n-k)
projectively embedded in
{\mathbb{P}}(\wedge^{k}V_{n})
by the Plücker embedding.
By
OG(k,V_{n})
we denote the orthogonal Grassmannian of
k
-dimensional linear spaces, i.e. the smooth projective variety parametrizing affine
k
-spaces which are isotropic with respect to a fixed nondegenerate symmetric form on
V_{n}
.
One can easily see that
OG(k,V_{n})
is cut by a general section of
\Sym^{2}{\mathcal{U}}^{\vee}
in
G(k,V_{n})
, where
{\mathcal{U}}
is the tautological bundle.
All orthogonal Grassmannians are connected except for
OG(n,V_{2n})
, which has two isomorphic connected components of dimension
n(n-1)/2
.
These are the varieties parametrizing isotropic
n
-spaces which intersect a fixed
n
-space in even (respectively odd) dimension. As usual, we denote by
spinor tenfold
each of the connected components of
OG(5,V_{10})
. In general, it is well-known that each connected component of
OG(n,V_{2n})
, also denoted by
OG(n,V_{2n})_{\pm}
, is isomorphic to
OG(n-1,V_{2n-1})
, where
V_{2n-1}
is a
2n-1
-dimensional vector space. This fact can be proven with classical techniques, see
[
Har92
, Theorem 22.14]
.

2.1.1.
A quick overview on homogeneous varieties

The following is standard material, which we recall for the purpose of notation and self-containedness: for a more exhaustive treatment see, for instance,
[
BE91
]
and the sources therein. Let us consider a simple Lie group
G
, with Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
. Fix a Cartan subalgebra
\mathfrak{h}\subset\mathfrak{g}
and call
\Delta
the set of roots induced by its adjoint action, with
\Delta_{+}\subset\Delta
the set of positive roots and
\Delta_{-}:=\Delta\setminus\Delta_{+}
. One has a root space decomposition for
\mathfrak{g}
:

\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{h}\oplus\bigoplus_{\alpha\in\Delta}\mathfrak{g}_{\alpha}

where the root eigenspaces
\mathfrak{g}_{\alpha}
are given by

\mathfrak{g}_{\alpha}=\{g\in\mathfrak{g}:[h,g]=\alpha(h)g\,\,\,\text{for}\,\,\,h\in\mathfrak{h}\}

Inside
\mathfrak{g}
, one distinguishes the
standard Borel subalgebra
given by the expression

\mathfrak{b}=\mathfrak{h}\oplus\mathfrak{n}

where
\mathfrak{n}=\bigoplus_{\alpha\in\Delta_{+}}\mathfrak{g}_{\alpha}
is a nilpotent summand.

A subalgebra
\mathfrak{p}\subset\mathfrak{g}
is called parabolic if it contains
\mathfrak{b}
as a subalgebra. Parabolic subalgebras can be characterized as follows.
Fix a set
{\mathcal{S}}=\{\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{r}\}
of simple roots, define subsets
{\mathcal{S}}_{i_{1}\dots i_{l}}:={\mathcal{S}}\setminus\{\alpha_{i_{1}},\dots,\alpha_{i_{l}}\}
and
\Delta_{i_{1}\dots i_{l}}=\operatorname{Span}({\mathcal{S}}_{i_{1}\dots i_{l}})\cap\Delta
. We consider the
levi subalgebras

\mathfrak{l}_{i_{1}\dots i_{l}}=\mathfrak{h}\oplus\bigoplus_{\alpha\in\Delta_{i_{1}\dots i_{l}}}\mathfrak{g}_{\alpha}

and some nilpotent subalgebras of the form

\mathfrak{u}_{{i_{1}\dots i_{l}}}=\bigoplus_{\alpha\in\Delta^{+}\setminus\Delta_{i_{1}\dots i_{l}}}\mathfrak{g}_{\alpha}

Then the direct sum

\mathfrak{p}_{{i_{1}\dots i_{l}}}=\mathfrak{l}_{{i_{1}\dots i_{l}}}\oplus\mathfrak{u}_{{i_{1}\dots i_{l}}}.

is a subalgebra and contains
\mathfrak{b}
, hence it is parabolic. The list of parabolic subalgebras
\{\mathfrak{p}_{i_{1},\dots,i_{l}}\}
is exhaustive up to conjugation.

If
\mathfrak{g}
is the Lie algebra of a simple Lie group
G
, it is well-known that a subgroup
P\subset G
is parabolic if and only if its Lie algebra is a parabolic subalgebra.
Hence, in the following, we will associate to a rational homogeneous variety
G/P
a marked Dynkin diagram.
Such a diagram corresponds to the choice of
G
, while the markings correspond to the set
{\mathcal{S}}_{i_{1}\dots i_{l}}
which fixes the parabolic subgroup (with respect to the choice of an ordering of simple roots).
We will also adopt the notation
G/P^{i_{1},\dots,i_{l}}
to describe the quotient of
G
by the parabolic subgroup whose parabolic subalgebra is
\mathfrak{p}_{i_{1},\dots,i_{l}}
.

2.1.2.
Homogeneous vector bundles over
G/P

It is a general fact that one has an equivalence of categories:

\operatorname{coh}^{G}(G/P)\simeq\operatorname{Rep}(P)

where on the left hand side we have the category of
G
-equivariant coherent sheaves on
G/P
and on the right hand side there is the category of finite dimensional representations of
P
.
Note that we can identify irreducible representations of
P
and
L
: in fact, any irreducible representation of
P
is determined by its restriction to
L
, since the unipotent radical of
P
acts trivially.
Thus, a homogeneous, irreducible vector bundle
{\mathcal{E}}_{\lambda}
on
G/P
is uniquely determined by the highest weight
\lambda
of a representation of
L
. More explicitly, we write:

{\mathcal{E}}_{\lambda}:=G\times^{P}{\mathbb{V}}^{P}_{\lambda}

where
{\mathbb{V}}^{P}_{\lambda}
is the vector space on which the
P
-representation of highest weight
\omega
acts, and with
G\times^{P}{\mathbb{V}}^{P}_{\lambda}
we denote the quotient of
G\times{\mathbb{V}}^{P}_{\lambda}
by the equivalence relation
(p.a,b)\sim(a,p.b)
for every
p\in P
and every
(a,b)\in G\times{\mathbb{V}}^{P}_{\lambda}
. Let us call
W_{L}
and
W_{G}
the Weyl groups of respectively
L
and
G
, and let us denote by
w_{0}^{L}
and
w_{0}^{G}
the respective longest elements. For later use, we recall that the Weyl reflections associated to simple roots act as follows:

(2.1)

S_{\alpha_{i}}:\alpha_{j}\longmapsto\alpha_{j}-A_{ij}\alpha_{i}

where
A
is the Cartan matrix. The following lemma is known to experts, see for instance
[
KP16
, Equation 8]
,
[
Smi22
, Lemma 2.1]

Lemma 2.1
.

One has:

(1)

({\mathcal{E}}_{\lambda})^{\vee}\simeq{\mathcal{E}}_{-w_{0}^{L}\lambda}

(2)

if
{\mathbb{V}}_{\lambda}^{L}\otimes{\mathbb{V}}_{\mu}^{L}=\bigoplus{\mathbb{V}}_{\nu}^{L}
, then
{\mathcal{E}}_{\lambda}\otimes{\mathcal{E}}_{\mu}=\bigoplus{\mathcal{E}}_{\nu}
.

2.1.3.
Homogeneous description of
OG(5,V_{10})_{+}

Consider the simple group of type
D_{5}
and its associated Dynkin diagram, with the ordering of the nodes chosen from left to right and from top to bottom for the last two (Bourbaki convention). We call
D_{5}/P^{i}
the generalized Grassmannians of type
D_{5}
. In particular, the two connected components
OG(5,V_{10})_{\pm}
of
OG(5,V_{10})
can be identified, respectively, with
G/P^{4}
and
G/P^{5}
. Hence, we can describe
OG(5,V_{10})_{+}
with the following marked Dynkin diagram:

The Levi subgroup is
SL(5)\times{\mathbb{C}}^{*}
.
Fixing an orthonormal basis
\{e_{1},\dots,e_{5}\}
of
{\mathbb{Q}}^{5}
, one can write the simple roots and the fundamental weights as follows:

\begin{split}\left.\begin{array}[]{cl}\alpha_{1}&=e_{1}-e_{2}\\[2.0pt]
\alpha_{2}&=e_{2}-e_{3}\\[2.0pt]
\alpha_{3}&=e_{3}-e_{4}\\[2.0pt]
\alpha_{4}&=e_{4}-e_{5}\\[2.0pt]
\alpha_{5}&=e_{4}+e_{5}.\end{array}\right.&\hskip 60.0pt\left.\begin{array}[]{cl}\omega_{1}&=e_{1}\\[2.0pt]
\omega_{2}&=e_{1}+e_{2}\\[2.0pt]
\omega_{3}&=e_{1}+e_{2}+e_{3}\\[2.0pt]
\omega_{4}&=\frac{1}{2}(e_{1}+e_{2}+e_{3}+e_{4}-e_{5})\\[2.0pt]
\omega_{5}&=\frac{1}{2}(e_{1}+e_{2}+e_{3}+e_{4}+e_{5}).\end{array}\right.\end{split}

Consider now a weight
\omega=\sum a_{i}\omega_{i}
. Then, by applying Equation
2.1
to the present setting, we find:

\left.\begin{array}[]{cl}S_{\alpha_{1}}\omega&=-a_{1}\omega_{1}+(a_{2}+a_{1})\omega_{2}+a_{3}\omega_{3}+a_{4}\omega_{4}+a_{5}\omega_{5}\\[2.0pt]
S_{\alpha_{2}}\omega&=(a_{1}+a_{2})\omega_{1}-a_{2}\omega_{2}+a_{3}\omega_{3}+a_{4}\omega_{4}+a_{5}\omega_{5}\\[2.0pt]
S_{\alpha_{3}}\omega&=a_{1}\omega_{1}+(a_{2}+a_{3})\omega_{2}-a_{3}\omega_{3}+(a_{4}+a_{3})\omega_{4}+(a_{5}+a_{3})\omega_{5}\\[2.0pt]
S_{\alpha_{4}}\omega&=a_{1}\omega_{1}+a_{2}\omega_{2}+(a_{3}+a_{4})\omega_{3}-a_{4}\omega_{4}+a_{5}\omega_{5}\\[2.0pt]
S_{\alpha_{5}}\omega&=a_{1}\omega_{1}+a_{2}\omega_{2}+(a_{3}+a_{5})\omega_{3}+a_{4}\omega_{4}-a_{5}\omega_{5}\\
\end{array}\right.

Call now
{\mathcal{U}}
the pullback to
OG(5,V_{10})_{+}
of the tautological bundle of
G(5,V_{10})
under the natural embedding given by the fact that every element of
OG(5,V_{10})_{+}
also belongs to
G(5,V_{10})
, and call
{\mathcal{O}}(1)
the pullback of the hyperplane bundle of
{\mathbb{P}}^{15}
under the spinor embedding (see
[
RS00
, Section 6]
for an explicit description of such embedding in a local chart). Note that this is
not
the pullback of
{\mathcal{O}}_{G(5,V_{10})}(1)
, which is
{\mathcal{O}}(2)
. One has, for every
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}
, for every positive
l\in{\mathbb{Z}}
and for
m=1,2,3
:

(2.2)

{\mathcal{O}}(k)={\mathcal{E}}_{k\omega_{4}}\hskip 30.0pt\Sym^{l}{\mathcal{U}}^{\vee}={\mathcal{E}}_{l\omega_{1}}\hskip 30.0pt\wedge^{m}{\mathcal{U}}^{\vee}={\mathcal{E}}_{\omega_{m}}\hskip 30.0pt\wedge^{4}{\mathcal{U}}^{\vee}={\mathcal{E}}_{\omega_{4}+\omega_{5}}.

Moreover, given a homogeneous irreducible vector bundle
{\mathcal{E}}_{\lambda}
, we have
{\mathcal{E}}_{\lambda}\otimes{\mathcal{O}}(k)={\mathcal{E}}_{\lambda+k\omega_{4}}
. Observe that
\omega_{1}
is dominant, and hence
{\mathcal{U}}^{\vee}
is globally generated. Its sections are computed in Lemma
A.2
to be
H^{\bullet}(OG(5,V_{10})_{+},{\mathcal{U}}^{\vee})={\mathbb{V}}^{D_{5}}_{\omega_{1}}\simeq{\mathbb{C}}^{10}
. On the other hand,
{\mathcal{U}}
has no cohomology (Lemma
A.7
). We can identify the pullback of the tautological sequence of
G(5,V_{10})
with the following:

(2.3)

0\longrightarrow{\mathcal{U}}\longrightarrow{\mathbb{V}}^{D_{5}}_{\omega_{1}}\otimes{\mathcal{O}}\longrightarrow{\mathcal{U}}^{\vee}\longrightarrow 0

where we used the standard identification
{\mathcal{U}}^{\vee}\simeq({\mathbb{V}}^{D_{5}}_{\omega_{1}}\otimes{\mathcal{O}})/{\mathcal{U}}
and
{\mathbb{V}}^{D_{5}}_{\omega_{1}}\simeq V_{10}
.

2.1.4.
Homogeneous description of
OG(4,V_{9})

Let us carry out a similar analysis for
OG(4,V_{9})
. To distinguish from the previous case, let us call
Q^{i}
the parabolic subgroups of the simple Lie group of type
B_{4}
, and
B_{4}/Q^{i}
the associated generalized Grassmannians. If we consider the Dynkin diagram of type
B_{4}
, with the ordering of simple roots from left to right, we have
OG(4,V_{9})=G/Q^{4}
, and the following marked Dynkin diagram:

In this case, the Levi subgroup is
SL(4)\times{\mathbb{C}}^{*}
. With respect to an orthonormal basis
\{\epsilon_{1},\dots,\epsilon_{4}\}
of
{\mathbb{Q}}^{4}
we can choose the simple roots and the fundamental weights to be:

\begin{split}\hskip 10.0pt\left.\begin{array}[]{cl}\beta_{1}&=\epsilon_{1}-\epsilon_{2}\\
\beta_{2}&=\epsilon_{2}-\epsilon_{3}\\
\beta_{3}&=\epsilon_{3}-\epsilon_{4}\\
\beta_{4}&=\epsilon_{4}.\end{array}\right.&\hskip 40.0pt\left.\begin{array}[]{cl}\omega_{1}&=\epsilon_{1}\\
\omega_{2}&=\epsilon_{1}+\epsilon_{2}\\
\omega_{3}&=\epsilon_{1}+\epsilon_{2}+\epsilon_{3}\\
\omega_{4}&=\epsilon_{1}+\epsilon_{2}+\epsilon_{3}+2\epsilon_{4}\end{array}\right.\end{split}

By Equation
2.1
, for a weight
\nu=\sum b_{i}\nu_{i}
, the Weyl reflections associated to the simple roots
\{\beta_{1},\dots,\beta_{4}\}
are:

\left.\begin{array}[]{cl}S_{\beta_{1}}\nu&=-b_{1}\nu_{1}+(b_{2}+b_{1})\nu_{2}+b_{3}\nu_{3}+b_{4}\nu_{4}\\[2.0pt]
S_{\beta_{2}}\nu&=(b_{1}+b_{2})\nu_{1}-b_{2}\nu_{2}+b_{3}\nu_{3}+b_{4}\nu_{4}\\[2.0pt]
S_{\beta_{3}}\nu&=b_{1}\nu_{1}+(b_{2}+b_{3})\nu_{2}-b_{3}\nu_{3}+(b_{4}+2b_{3})\nu_{4}\\[2.0pt]
S_{\beta_{4}}\nu&=b_{1}\nu_{1}+b_{2}\nu_{2}+(b_{3}+b_{4})\nu_{3}-b_{4}\nu_{4}\end{array}\right.

Denote by
{\mathcal{R}}
the pullback to
OG(4,V_{9})
of the tautological bundle of
G(4,V_{9})
and, as above, call
{\mathcal{O}}(1)
the pullback of the hyperplane bundle of
{\mathbb{P}}^{15}
. This again is not the pullback of
{\mathcal{O}}_{G(4,V_{9})}(1)
, which is
{\mathcal{O}}(2)
. One has, for every
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}
, for every positive
l\in{\mathbb{Z}}
and for
m=1,2,3
:

(2.4)

{\mathcal{O}}(k)={\mathcal{E}}_{k\nu_{4}}\hskip 30.0pt\Sym^{l}{\mathcal{R}}^{\vee}={\mathcal{E}}_{l\nu_{1}}\hskip 30.0pt\wedge^{m}{\mathcal{R}}^{\vee}={\mathcal{E}}_{\nu_{m}}.

Pulling back the tautological sequence of
G(4,V_{9})
we find:

(2.5)

0\longrightarrow{\mathcal{R}}\longrightarrow{\mathbb{V}}^{B_{4}}_{\nu_{1}}\otimes{\mathcal{O}}\longrightarrow{\mathcal{W}}\longrightarrow 0

where
{\mathbb{V}}^{B_{4}}_{\nu_{1}}\simeq V_{9}
.

2.2.
Some exact sequences

The tautological bundles of the spinor tenfold with respect to its dual homogeneous description, and their Schur powers, interact in an interesting way giving rise to some exact sequences which will be useful in the next section. Let us describe how they can be constructed. Once for all, we set
X:=OG(5,V_{10})_{+}\simeq OG(4,V_{9})
.

Lemma 2.2
.

There is a short exact sequence on
X
:

(2.6)

0\longrightarrow{\mathcal{R}}\longrightarrow{\mathcal{U}}\longrightarrow{\mathcal{O}}\longrightarrow 0

Moreover, one has an isomorphism
{\mathcal{W}}\simeq{\mathcal{U}}^{\vee}
.

Proof.

One has
\operatorname{Ext}^{1}({\mathcal{O}},{\mathcal{R}})\simeq H^{1}(X,{\mathcal{R}})\simeq{\mathbb{C}}
by Corollary
A.13
. Hence, up to isomorphisms, there exists a unique nontrivial extension of
{\mathcal{R}}
by
{\mathcal{O}}
. Let us call
E
such a nontrivial extension, and consider the short exact sequence:

(2.7)

0\longrightarrow{\mathcal{R}}\longrightarrow E\longrightarrow{\mathcal{O}}\longrightarrow 0.

By applying the functor
\operatorname{Hom}(-,{\mathcal{U}})
we have:

0\longrightarrow\operatorname{Hom}({\mathcal{O}},{\mathcal{U}})\longrightarrow\operatorname{Hom}(E,{\mathcal{U}})\longrightarrow\operatorname{Hom}({\mathcal{R}},{\mathcal{U}})\longrightarrow\operatorname{Ext}^{1}({\mathcal{O}},{\mathcal{U}})\longrightarrow\cdots

and since by Lemma
A.7
the vector bundle
{\mathcal{U}}
has no cohomology, the middle arrow is an isomorphism. Note that one can construct a nontrivial morphism
{\mathcal{R}}\longrightarrow{\mathcal{U}}
by an embedding
V_{9}\subset V_{10}
together with the fact that an isotropic
4
-space in
V_{10}
can be completed to an isotropic
5
-space of
OG_{+}(5,10)
in exactly one way: hence there is at least a nonzero map
E\longrightarrow{\mathcal{U}}
. If both bundles are stable and they have the same slope, then the map must be an isomorphism: they have the same slope (both are extensions of
{\mathcal{O}}
and
{\mathcal{R}}
), and
{\mathcal{U}}
is stable because it is homogeneous and irreducible, let us prove that
E
is stable as well. By Hoppe’s criterion (see
[
JMPSE17
]
and its application in
[
Man19
]
),
E
is stable if the following bundles have no global sections:

E,\hskip 15.0pt\wedge^{2}E,\hskip 15.0pt\wedge^{3}E(1),\hskip 15.0pt\wedge^{4}E(1).

Observe that, comparing the
r
-th and the
(r-1)
-rth exterior powers of the sequence
2.7
, one finds a short exact sequence:

0\longrightarrow\wedge^{r}{\mathcal{R}}\longrightarrow\wedge^{r}E\longrightarrow\wedge^{r-1}{\mathcal{R}}\longrightarrow 0.

By this and Lemma
A.14
we immediately see that
\wedge^{2}E
,
\wedge^{3}E(1)
and
\wedge^{4}E(1)
have no sections (for the last we also use the fact that
\wedge^{4}{\mathcal{R}}(1)\simeq{\mathcal{O}}(-1)
).

Let us now prove that
E
has no global sections.
Assume it had: then
H^{0}(X,E)\simeq H^{1}(X,E)\simeq H^{0}(X,{\mathcal{O}})\simeq{\mathbb{C}}
because of the long exact sequence of cohomologies of the sequence
2.7
.
Moreover, since by the sequence
2.7
the top chern class of
E
is zero, we have both an injective map
{\mathcal{O}}\longrightarrow E
and a surjection
E\longrightarrow{\mathcal{O}}
(the latter again from
2.7
). The composition of these maps is an element of
\operatorname{Hom}({\mathcal{O}},{\mathcal{O}})\simeq{\mathbb{C}}
and hence a multiple of the identity (nonvanishing by the exactness of
2.7
): this implies that the sequence
2.7
splits, providing a contradiction.
Thus, by Hoppe’s criterion we conclude that
E
is stable, and since it has the same slope of
{\mathcal{U}}
and there exists a nonzero morphism
{\mathcal{U}}\longrightarrow E
, they are isomorphic, concluding the proof of the first claim.

Let us now turn our attention to the second claim. One has a commutative diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{R}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V_{9}\otimes{\mathcal{O}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{W}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{U}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V_{10}\otimes{\mathcal{O}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{U}}^{\vee}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{O}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{O}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}

and by the snake lemma, we conclude that
\phi
is an isomorphism.
∎

The following lemma, and its proof, are already known to experts (see the MathOverflow thread
[
Mat
]
for instance):

Lemma 2.3
.

The tangent bundle
T_{X}
is the unique extension, up to isomorphism, fitting in the following exact sequence:

(2.8)

0\longrightarrow{\mathcal{R}}^{\vee}\longrightarrow T_{X}\longrightarrow\wedge^{2}{\mathcal{R}}^{\vee}\longrightarrow 0.

Moreover, one has
T_{X}\simeq\wedge^{2}{\mathcal{U}}^{\vee}
.

Proof.

Since
X
is the zero locus of a general section of
\Sym^{2}{\mathcal{R}}^{\vee}
in
G(4,V_{9})
, it comes with a normal bundle sequence:

0\longrightarrow T_{X}\longrightarrow{\mathcal{R}}^{\vee}\otimes{\mathcal{W}}\longrightarrow\Sym^{2}{\mathcal{R}}^{\vee}\longrightarrow 0.

Together with the sequence
2.6
, this gives rise to a diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{coker}\phi}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T_{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{R}}^{\vee}\otimes{\mathcal{W}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sym^{2}{\mathcal{R}}^{\vee}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{R}}^{\vee}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{R}}^{\vee}\otimes{\mathcal{U}}^{\vee}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{R}}^{\vee}\otimes{\mathcal{R}}^{\vee}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ker\phi}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\wedge^{2}{\mathcal{R}}^{\vee}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}

from which the existence of the sequence
2.8
follows by the snake lemma. Uniqueness is a consequence of the following computation:

\begin{split}\operatorname{Ext}^{1}(\wedge^{2}{\mathcal{R}}^{\vee},{\mathcal{R}}^{\vee})&\simeq H^{1}(X,\wedge^{2}{\mathcal{R}}\otimes{\mathcal{R}}^{\vee})\\
&\simeq H^{1}(X,\wedge^{2}{\mathcal{R}}^{\vee}\otimes{\mathcal{R}}^{\vee}(-2))\\
&\simeq H^{1}(X,\wedge^{3}{\mathcal{R}}^{\vee}(-2))\oplus H^{1}(X,{\mathcal{E}}_{\nu_{1}+\nu_{2}-2\nu_{4}}).\end{split}

Here by Lemma
A.11
the first summand is isomorphic to
{\mathbb{C}}
and the second is trivial, and this proves that there is only one isomorphism class of extensions of
{\mathcal{R}}^{\vee}
and
\wedge^{2}{\mathcal{R}}^{\vee}
, concluding the proof of the first claim.

To prove the second statement, let us call
{\mathcal{U}}_{G(5,V_{10})}
and
{\mathcal{Q}}_{G(5,V_{10})}
the tautological and quotient bundles of
G(5,V_{10})
. Observe that
{\mathcal{Q}}_{G(5,V_{10})}
restricts to
{\mathcal{U}}^{\vee}
on
X
, and therefore
T_{G(5,V_{10})}|_{X}\simeq{\mathcal{U}}^{\vee}\otimes{\mathcal{U}}^{\vee}
. Then, observe that there is a unique map
{\mathcal{U}}^{\vee}\otimes{\mathcal{U}}^{\vee}\longrightarrow\Sym^{2}{\mathcal{U}}^{\vee}
up to scalar multiple, and such a map is surjective with kernel
\wedge^{2}{\mathcal{U}}^{\vee}
. Since
{\mathcal{N}}_{X|G(5,V_{10})}\simeq\Sym^{2}{\mathcal{U}}^{\vee}
, we conclude that
T_{X}\simeq\wedge^{2}{\mathcal{U}}^{\vee}
by the normal bundle sequence of
X\subset G(5,V_{10})
.
∎

Recall the
affine tangent bundle

\widehat{T}_{X}(1)
, which is an extension of the form

(2.9)

0\longrightarrow{\mathcal{O}}(-1)\longrightarrow\widehat{T}_{X}\longrightarrow T_{X}(-1)\longrightarrow 0.

Such extension is unique because
h^{1}(X,T_{X})=1
(
X
is rigid).

Lemma 2.4
.

There is a short exact sequence:

(2.10)

0\longrightarrow\widehat{T}_{X}\longrightarrow{\mathbb{V}}^{D_{5}}_{\omega_{4}}\otimes{\mathcal{O}}\longrightarrow{\mathcal{U}}(1)\longrightarrow 0.

Proof.

By a simple calculation, it is easy to see that
{\mathcal{U}}(1)
is globally generated. In fact, one has
{\mathcal{U}}(1)\simeq\wedge^{4}{\mathcal{U}}^{\vee}(1)
, and since
\wedge^{4}{\mathcal{U}}^{\vee}={\mathcal{E}}_{\omega_{4}+\omega_{5}}
and
{\mathcal{O}}(-1)={\mathcal{E}}_{-\omega_{5}}
we have
{\mathcal{U}}(1)\simeq{\mathcal{E}}_{\omega_{4}}
. Now,
\omega_{4}
is a dominant weight and therefore
{\mathcal{U}}(1)
is globally generated, with
H^{0}(X,{\mathcal{U}}(1))={\mathbb{V}}^{D_{5}}_{\omega_{4}}\simeq{\mathbb{C}}^{16}
. Let us define
{\mathcal{F}}_{11}
as the kernel of the evaluation map on sections of
{\mathcal{U}}(1)
, i.e.

(2.11)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{F}}_{11}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{0}(X,{\mathcal{U}}(1))\otimes{\mathcal{O}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{U}}(1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{ev}}

The rest of the proof consists in showing that
\widehat{T}_{X}\simeq{\mathcal{F}}_{11}(1)
.
In the following diagram, the horizontal rows are respectively the sequence
2.11
and the normal bundle sequence of
X\subset{\mathbb{P}}^{15}
, while the central vertical row is the restriction to
X
of the Euler sequence of the ambient
{\mathbb{P}}^{15}
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ker\phi}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{O}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{F}}_{11}(1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{V}}^{D_{5}}_{\omega_{4}}\otimes{\mathcal{O}}(1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{U}}(2)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T_{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T_{{\mathbb{P}}^{15}}|_{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{U}}(2)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{coker}\phi}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}

By the snake lemma we deduce that
\phi
is surjective with kernel
{\mathcal{O}}
. Recall that extensions of the type described in the sequence
2.9
are unique up to isomorphism: thus,
\widehat{T}_{X}\simeq{\mathcal{F}}_{11}(1)
.
∎

Lemma 2.5
.

There is the following exact sequence:

0\longrightarrow{\mathcal{U}}^{\vee}\longrightarrow{\mathbb{V}}^{D_{5}}_{\omega_{4}}\otimes{\mathcal{O}}(1)\longrightarrow{\mathbb{V}}^{D_{5}}_{\omega_{1}}\otimes{\mathcal{U}}(2)\longrightarrow{\mathbb{V}}^{D_{5}}_{2\omega_{1}}\otimes{\mathcal{O}}(2)\longrightarrow\Sym^{2}{\mathcal{U}}^{\vee}(2)\longrightarrow 0

Proof.

First, let us prove the existence of a sequence

(2.12)

0\longrightarrow\widehat{T}_{X}^{\vee}(-1)\longrightarrow{\mathbb{V}}^{D_{5}}_{\omega_{1}}\otimes{\mathcal{U}}\longrightarrow{\mathbb{V}}^{D_{5}}_{2\omega_{1}}\otimes{\mathcal{O}}\longrightarrow\Sym^{2}{\mathcal{U}}^{\vee}\longrightarrow 0.

To do so, we start by applying the
\Sym^{2}
-Schur functor to the tautological exact sequence of
X
(as a
D_{5}
-Grassmannian), obtaining:

0\longrightarrow\wedge^{2}{\mathcal{U}}\longrightarrow{\mathbb{V}}^{D_{5}}_{\omega_{1}}\otimes{\mathcal{U}}\longrightarrow\Sym^{2}{\mathbb{V}}^{D_{5}}_{\omega_{1}}\otimes{\mathcal{O}}\longrightarrow\Sym^{2}{\mathcal{U}}^{\vee}\longrightarrow 0.

Note that
\Sym^{2}{\mathbb{V}}^{D_{5}}_{\omega_{1}}\simeq{\mathbb{V}}^{D_{5}}_{2\omega_{1}}\oplus{\mathbb{C}}
. Hence we write the following diagram, where rows and columns are exact,
{\mathcal{K}}
and
\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}
denote the kernels of the two rightmost horizontal maps and
{\mathcal{C}}
is the cokernel of the central map of the second row:

(2.13)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{K}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{O}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\wedge^{2}{\mathcal{U}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{V}}^{D_{5}}_{\omega_{1}}\otimes{\mathcal{U}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{V}}^{D_{5}}_{2\omega_{1}}\otimes{\mathcal{O}}\oplus{\mathcal{O}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sym^{2}{\mathcal{U}}^{\vee}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{T}_{X}^{\vee}(-1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{V}}^{D_{5}}_{\omega_{1}}\otimes{\mathcal{U}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{V}}^{D_{5}}_{2\omega_{1}}\otimes{\mathcal{O}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{C}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{O}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\exists}

The dashed arrow, which makes the rightmost square commutative, exists because if we apply the functor
\operatorname{Hom}(-,{\mathbb{V}}^{D_{5}}_{\omega_{1}}\otimes{\mathcal{U}})
to the leftmost column we obtain a long exact sequence of
\operatorname{Ext}
-groups, and since
\operatorname{Ext}^{\bullet}({\mathcal{O}},{\mathcal{U}})=0
we find an isomorphism
\operatorname{Hom}(\widehat{T}_{X}^{\vee}(-1),{\mathbb{V}}^{D_{5}}_{\omega_{1}}\otimes{\mathcal{U}})\simeq\operatorname{Hom}(\wedge^{2}{\mathcal{U}},{\mathbb{V}}^{D_{5}}_{\omega_{1}}\otimes{\mathcal{U}})
. Then we can apply the snake lemma to the two leftmost vertical exact sequences, and this yields a short exact sequence

0\longrightarrow{\mathcal{O}}\longrightarrow{\mathcal{K}}\longrightarrow\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}\longrightarrow 0.

Hence, we can write the following diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{O}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{K}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{O}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{V}}^{D_{5}}_{2\omega_{1}}\otimes{\mathcal{O}}\oplus{\mathcal{O}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{V}}^{D_{5}}_{2\omega_{1}}\otimes{\mathcal{O}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sym^{2}{\mathcal{U}}^{\vee}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{C}}}

By the snake lemma we conclude that
{\mathcal{C}}\simeq\Sym^{2}{\mathcal{U}}^{\vee}
, which gives us the sequence
2.12
.

By Lemma
A.10
one has
\operatorname{Ext}(\Sym^{2}{\mathcal{U}}^{\vee},\widehat{T}_{X}^{\vee}(-1))={\mathbb{C}}[-2]
: hence, there exists a unique two-terms extension of the form

0\longrightarrow\widehat{T}_{X}^{\vee}(1)\longrightarrow B\longrightarrow C\longrightarrow\Sym^{2}{\mathcal{U}}^{\vee}(2)\longrightarrow 0.

On the other hand, we see that
\operatorname{Ext}(\widehat{T}_{X}^{\vee}(1),{\mathcal{U}}^{\vee})={\mathbb{C}}[-1]
: this follows by the fact that by twisting the sequence
2.10
we obtain:

0\longrightarrow{\mathcal{U}}^{\vee}\otimes\widehat{T}_{X}(-1)\longrightarrow{\mathbb{V}}^{D_{5}}_{\omega_{1}}\otimes{\mathcal{U}}^{\vee}(-1)\longrightarrow{\mathcal{U}}^{\vee}\otimes{\mathcal{U}}\longrightarrow 0,

where the middle term has no cohomology by Lemma
A.5
and the last one contributes with
{\mathbb{C}}[0]
because
{\mathcal{U}}
is exceptional. Thus, there is a unique
A
, up to isomorphism, fitting in the following exact sequence:

0\longrightarrow{\mathcal{U}}^{\vee}\longrightarrow A\longrightarrow\widehat{T}_{X}^{\vee}(1)\longrightarrow 0.

By composing the last two equations, respectively, with appropriate twists of the duals of
2.11
and
2.9
, we find two long exact sequences:

(2.14)

0\longrightarrow{\mathcal{U}}^{\vee}\longrightarrow{\mathbb{V}}^{D_{5}}_{\omega_{4}}\otimes{\mathcal{O}}(1)\longrightarrow B\longrightarrow C\longrightarrow\Sym^{2}{\mathcal{U}}^{\vee}(2)\longrightarrow 0.

(2.15)

0\longrightarrow{\mathcal{U}}^{\vee}\longrightarrow A\longrightarrow{\mathbb{V}}^{D_{5}}_{\omega_{1}}\otimes{\mathcal{U}}(2)\longrightarrow{\mathbb{V}}^{D_{5}}_{2\omega_{1}}\otimes{\mathcal{O}}(2)\longrightarrow\Sym^{2}{\mathcal{U}}^{\vee}(2)\longrightarrow 0

Now, by Lemma
A.9
there is a unique three-terms extension between
{\mathcal{U}}^{\vee}
and
\Sym^{2}{\mathcal{U}}^{\vee}(2)
which is not a direct sum of shorter extensions, and therefore the sequences
2.14
and
2.15
must coincide up to isomorphisms, and this concludes the proof.
∎

3.
The KP collection on the spinor tenfold

Let
G
be a simple Lie group of type
B_{4}
. By
D^{b}_{G}(X)
we denote the equivariant category of coherent sheaves on the spinor tenfold
X
. We will use the notation
1
1

1

Note that this choice of notation differs from the one of Kuznetsov and Polishchuk.

{\mathcal{E}}_{\lambda}
for both the homogeneous vector bundle
G\times^{P}{\mathbb{V}}^{P}_{\lambda}
in the category
D^{b}_{G}(X)
of equivariant sheaves, and its image through the forgetful functor
\operatorname{Fg}:D^{b}_{G}(X)\longrightarrow D^{b}(X)
.
We also fix the shorthand notation:

\begin{split}\operatorname{Ext}_{G}^{\bullet}(-,-)&:=\operatorname{Ext}_{D^{b}_{G}(X)}^{\bullet}(-,-)\\
\operatorname{Ext}^{\bullet}(-,-)&:=\operatorname{Ext}^{\bullet}_{D^{b}(X)}(-,-).\end{split}

As usual, given a vector space
W
with a
G
-action, we call
W^{G}\subset W
the space of
G
-invariants.

By the property
\operatorname{Ext}_{G}^{\bullet}({\mathcal{E}}_{\lambda},{\mathcal{E}}_{\mu})=\operatorname{Ext}^{\bullet}({\mathcal{E}}_{\lambda},{\mathcal{E}}_{\mu})^{G}

[
KP16
, Proposition 2.17]
, one has the remarkable property that every homogeneous, irreducible vector bundle is exceptional in
D^{b}_{G}(X)
. For the standard definitions of semiorthogonal decomposition, exceptional object and full exceptional collection, we refer to
[
Kuz16
, Section 2]
.

3.1.
Mutations of exceptional objects

Let
{\mathcal{T}}
be a triangulated category, and
{\mathcal{A}}_{1},\dots,{\mathcal{A}}_{n}
admissible subcategories such that
{\mathcal{T}}=\langle{\mathcal{A}}_{1},\dots,{\mathcal{A}}_{n}\rangle
is a semiorthogonal decomposition. Mutations are a well-established technique for producing other semiorthogonal decompositions from a given one (see
[
Bon90
,
BO02
]
). The setting of the problem we address in this paper is much more specific: let
Y
be a smooth projective variety and
D^{b}(Y)=\langle E_{1},\dots,E_{n}\rangle
a full exceptional collection of vector bundles. Then, all the techniques we need to recall about mutations can be summarized in the following two lemmas, which are well-known to experts.

Lemma 3.1
.

Let
Y
be a smooth projective variety and
D^{b}(Y)=\langle E_{1},\dots,E_{n}\rangle
a full exceptional collection. Then, one has:

\begin{split}D^{b}(Y)&=\langle E_{1},\dots,E_{i-1},E_{i+1},{\mathbb{R}}_{E_{i+1}}E_{i},E_{i+2},\dots,E_{n}\rangle\\
&=\langle E_{1},\dots,E_{i-1},{\mathbb{L}}_{E_{i}}E_{i+1},E_{i},E_{i+2},\dots,E_{n}\rangle\end{split}

where:

\begin{split}{\mathbb{R}}_{E_{i+1}}E_{i}&=\operatorname{Cone}(E_{i}\longrightarrow E_{i+1}\otimes\operatorname{Ext}_{\mathcal{T}}^{\bullet}(E_{i},E_{i+1})^{\vee})[-1]\\
{\mathbb{L}}_{E_{i}}E_{i+1}&=\operatorname{Cone}(E_{i}\otimes\operatorname{Ext}_{\mathcal{T}}^{\bullet}(E_{i},E_{i+1})\longrightarrow E_{i+1})\end{split}

with the morphisms being respectively the evaluation and coevaluation maps.

Lemma 3.2
.

Consider a full exceptional collection
D^{b}(Y)=\langle E_{1},\dots,E_{n}\rangle
for
Y
smooth projective. Then, up to a shift, the following holds:

(1)

If there is a short exact sequence
0\longrightarrow F\longrightarrow V\otimes E_{1}\longrightarrow E_{2}\longrightarrow 0
, and
\operatorname{Ext}_{D^{b}(Y)}^{\bullet}(E_{1},E_{2})=V[0]
, then
{\mathbb{L}}_{E_{1}}E_{2}\simeq F
.

(2)

If there is a short exact sequence
0\longrightarrow E_{1}\longrightarrow V\otimes E_{2}\longrightarrow F\longrightarrow 0
, and
\operatorname{Ext}_{D^{b}(Y)}^{\bullet}(E_{1},E_{2})=V[0]
, then
{\mathbb{R}}_{E_{2}}E_{1}\simeq F
.

(3)

If there is a short exact sequence
0\longrightarrow E_{2}\longrightarrow F\longrightarrow E_{1}\longrightarrow 0
, and
\operatorname{Ext}_{D^{b}(Y)}^{\bullet}(E_{1},E_{2})={\mathbb{C}}[-1]
, then
{\mathbb{R}}_{E_{2}}E_{1}\simeq{\mathbb{L}}_{E_{1}}E_{2}\simeq F
.

We also recall, in our setting, the definition of a right dual exceptional collection (as given in
[
KP16
]
).

Definition 3.3
.

Given
D^{b}(Y)=\langle E_{1},\dots,E_{n}\rangle
as above, we define the associated
\emph{rightdual}
collection as follows:

\langle E_{n},{\mathbb{R}}_{E_{n}}E_{n-1},\dots,{\mathbb{R}}_{\ \langle E_{2},\dots,E_{n}\rangle}E_{1}\rangle.

3.2.
The forgetful functor and the exceptional blocks

Let us apply the prescription of
[
KP16
, Theorem 9.3]
to
G/P=X
to produce a list of subcategories
{\mathcal{A}}_{0},\dots,{\mathcal{A}}_{7}
of
D^{b}_{G}(X)
, each generated by exceptional objects. We find:

\begin{split}{\mathcal{A}}_{0}&=\langle{\mathcal{O}}\rangle_{G}\\
{\mathcal{A}}_{1}&=\langle{\mathcal{O}}(1)\rangle_{G}\\
{\mathcal{A}}_{2}&=\langle\Sym^{2}{\mathcal{R}}^{\vee}(2),{\mathcal{R}}^{\vee}(2),{\mathcal{O}}(2)\rangle_{G}\\
{\mathcal{A}}_{3}&=\langle\Sym^{2}{\mathcal{R}}^{\vee}(3),{\mathcal{R}}^{\vee}(3),{\mathcal{O}}(3)\rangle_{G}\\
{\mathcal{A}}_{4}&=\langle\wedge^{2}{\mathcal{R}}^{\vee}(4),{\mathcal{R}}^{\vee}(4),{\mathcal{O}}(4)\rangle_{G}\\
{\mathcal{A}}_{5}&=\langle\wedge^{2}{\mathcal{R}}^{\vee}(5),{\mathcal{R}}^{\vee}(5),{\mathcal{O}}(5)\rangle_{G}\\
{\mathcal{A}}_{6}&=\langle{\mathcal{O}}(6)\rangle_{G}\\
{\mathcal{A}}_{7}&=\langle{\mathcal{O}}(7)\rangle_{G}\\
\end{split}

where
\langle\quad\rangle_{G}
represents an exceptional collection in
D^{b}_{G}(X)
. Note that a naïve application of the forgetful functor on each generator of the
{\mathcal{A}}_{i}
’s does
not
produce an exceptional collection in
D^{b}(X)
.
According to
[
KP16
, Section 3.3]
, a possible way to produce an exceptional collection for
X
is the following: first we take the right dual
{\mathcal{B}}_{i}
of the block
{\mathcal{A}}_{i}
, and we apply the forgetful functor to each generator of each block
{\mathcal{B}}_{i}
. Finally, we consider the collection
\langle\operatorname{Fg}{\mathcal{B}}_{0},\dots,\operatorname{Fg}{\mathcal{B}}_{7}\rangle
.
This task is carried out in the following of this section.

Lemma 3.4
.

There is the following equivalence of categories:

\operatorname{Fg}\langle\wedge^{2}{\mathcal{R}}^{\vee},{\mathcal{R}}^{\vee},{\mathcal{O}}\rangle_{G}\simeq\langle{\mathcal{O}},{\mathcal{U}}^{\vee},\widehat{T}_{X}(1)\rangle.

Proof.

Following
[
KP16
, Section 3.3]
we can produce a full exceptional collection for
\operatorname{Fg}\langle\wedge^{2}{\mathcal{R}}^{\vee},{\mathcal{R}}^{\vee},{\mathcal{O}}\rangle_{G}
by constructing the right dual collection to
\langle\wedge^{2}{\mathcal{R}}^{\vee},{\mathcal{R}}^{\vee},{\mathcal{O}}\rangle_{G}
,
in the equivariant category
, and then applying the forgetful functor on each generator. In other words, we only need to show that the right dual of
\langle\wedge^{2}{\mathcal{R}}^{\vee},{\mathcal{R}}^{\vee},{\mathcal{O}}\rangle_{G}
is
\langle{\mathcal{O}},{\mathcal{U}}^{\vee},\widehat{T}_{X}(1)\rangle_{G}
. Observe that, by Lemma
2.3
and
A.12
we can apply Lemma
3.2
to obtain
{\mathbb{R}}_{{\mathcal{R}}^{\vee}}\wedge^{2}{\mathcal{R}}^{\vee}=T_{X}
.
Now, let us mutate the result one step further to the right. The relevant Ext is
\operatorname{Ext}_{G}^{\bullet}(T_{X},{\mathcal{O}})={\mathbb{C}}[-1]
. Then, in light of the sequence
2.9
, we conclude by Lemma
3.2
that
{\mathbb{R}}_{\langle{\mathcal{R}}^{\vee},{\mathcal{O}}\rangle}\wedge^{2}{\mathcal{R}}^{\vee}\simeq\widehat{T}_{X}(1)
. Similarly, by Lemma
2.2
(and in particular by the computations in its proof) we have
{\mathbb{R}}_{\mathcal{O}}{\mathcal{R}}^{\vee}\simeq{\mathcal{U}}^{\vee}
. This concludes the proof.
∎

Lemma 3.5
.

There is the following equivalence of categories:

\operatorname{Fg}\langle\Sym^{2}{\mathcal{R}}^{\vee},{\mathcal{R}}^{\vee},{\mathcal{O}}\rangle_{G}\simeq\langle{\mathcal{O}},{\mathcal{U}}^{\vee},\Sym^{2}{\mathcal{U}}^{\vee}\rangle.

Proof.

As above, we only need to prove that the right dual of
\langle\Sym^{2}{\mathcal{R}}^{\vee},{\mathcal{R}}^{\vee},{\mathcal{O}}\rangle_{G}
is
\langle{\mathcal{O}},{\mathcal{U}}^{\vee},\Sym^{2}{\mathcal{U}}^{\vee}\rangle_{G}
. Recall that mutations do not depend on the choice of the exceptional collection of the category we are mutating through. Hence, by the proof of the previous lemma, we have
{\mathbb{R}}_{\langle{\mathcal{R}}^{\vee},{\mathcal{O}}\rangle}\Sym^{2}{\mathcal{R}}^{\vee}={\mathbb{R}}_{\langle{\mathcal{O}},{\mathcal{U}}^{\vee}\rangle}\Sym^{2}{\mathcal{R}}^{\vee}
.
The right hand side of this last equation can be computed as follows: we first note that
\operatorname{Ext}_{G}(\Sym^{2}{\mathcal{R}}^{\vee},{\mathcal{O}})=0
. In fact, one has
\operatorname{Ext}_{G}(\Sym^{2}{\mathcal{R}}^{\vee},{\mathcal{O}})=H^{\bullet}(X,\Sym^{2}{\mathcal{R}})^{G}
, and the latter vanishes since
H^{\bullet}(X,\Sym^{2}{\mathcal{R}})
by Lemma
A.15
. Finally, we are left to compute
{\mathbb{R}}_{{\mathcal{U}}^{\vee}}\Sym^{2}{\mathcal{R}}^{\vee}
.

By taking the second exterior power of the dual of the sequence
2.6
we get:

0\longrightarrow{\mathcal{U}}^{\vee}\longrightarrow\Sym^{2}{\mathcal{U}}^{\vee}\longrightarrow\Sym^{2}{\mathcal{R}}^{\vee}\longrightarrow 0,

and since one has
\operatorname{Ext}_{G}^{\bullet}(\Sym^{2}{\mathcal{R}}^{\vee},{\mathcal{U}}^{\vee})=H^{\bullet}(X,\Sym^{2}{\mathcal{R}}\otimes{\mathcal{U}}^{\vee})^{G}={\mathbb{C}}[-1]
by Lemma
A.16
, we are able to conclude that
{\mathbb{R}}_{{\mathcal{U}}^{\vee}}\Sym^{2}{\mathcal{R}}^{\vee}\simeq\Sym^{2}{\mathcal{U}}^{\vee}
.
∎

Summing all up, we find:

Proposition 3.6
.

The following collection is exceptional in
D^{b}(X)
:

\begin{split}{\mathcal{A}}=\langle&{\mathcal{O}},{\mathcal{O}}(1),{\mathcal{O}}(2),{\mathcal{U}}^{\vee}(2),\Sym^{2}{\mathcal{U}}^{\vee}(2),{\mathcal{O}}(3),{\mathcal{U}}^{\vee}(3),\Sym^{2}{\mathcal{U}}^{\vee}(3),\\
&\hskip 40.0pt{\mathcal{O}}(4),{\mathcal{U}}^{\vee}(4),\widehat{T}_{X}(5),{\mathcal{O}}(5),{\mathcal{U}}^{\vee}(5),\widehat{T}_{X}(6),{\mathcal{O}}(6),{\mathcal{O}}(7)\rangle\end{split}

Remark 3.7
.

The presence of the affine tangent bundle in exceptional collections of homogeneous varieties has been investigated in
[
Smi22
, Observation 1.4]
, where the author observes the relation with the fact that the tangent bundle is a homogeneous, irreducible vector bundle, and therefore it is exceptional in the equivariant category. This fact is true only for cominuscule varieties (like the spinor tenfold).

3.3.
Fullness

Hereafter we describe the main result of this paper (Theorem
3.8
), which is the fullness of the KP collection for
X
we constructed in Proposition
3.6
. In particular, we describe a sequence of mutations which, applied to the KP collection, yield the full exceptional collection constructed by Kuznetsov in
[
Kuz06
]
, thus proving that the KP collection is full as well.

Proposition 3.8
.

There is an equivalence of categories:

\begin{split}\langle&{\mathcal{O}},{\mathcal{O}}(1),{\mathcal{O}}(2),{\mathcal{U}}^{\vee}(2),\Sym^{2}{\mathcal{U}}^{\vee}(2),{\mathcal{O}}(3),{\mathcal{U}}^{\vee}(3),\Sym^{2}{\mathcal{U}}^{\vee}(3),\\
&\hskip 40.0pt{\mathcal{O}}(4),{\mathcal{U}}^{\vee}(4),\widehat{T}_{X}(5),{\mathcal{O}}(5),{\mathcal{U}}^{\vee}(5),\widehat{T}_{X}(6),{\mathcal{O}}(6),{\mathcal{O}}(7)\rangle\\
\simeq\langle&{\mathcal{O}},{\mathcal{U}}^{\vee},{\mathcal{O}}(1),{\mathcal{U}}^{\vee}(1),{\mathcal{O}}(2),{\mathcal{U}}^{\vee}(2),{\mathcal{O}}(3),{\mathcal{U}}^{\vee}(3),\\
&\hskip 40.0pt{\mathcal{O}}(4),{\mathcal{U}}^{\vee}(4),{\mathcal{O}}(5),{\mathcal{U}}^{\vee}(5),{\mathcal{O}}(6),{\mathcal{U}}^{\vee}(6),{\mathcal{O}}(7),{\mathcal{U}}^{\vee}(7)\rangle\\
\end{split}

Proof.

Let us first describe how we can mutate
\widehat{T}_{X}(6)
one step to the right.
In light of Lemmas
3.2
and
2.4
, we have
{\mathbb{R}}_{{\mathcal{O}}(6)}\widehat{T}_{X}(6)\simeq{\mathcal{U}}^{\vee}(7)
if we can prove that
\operatorname{Ext}^{\bullet}(\widehat{T}_{X}(6),{\mathcal{O}}(6))\simeq{\mathbb{V}}^{D_{5}}_{\omega_{4}}[0]
, and the latter follows by Corollary
A.6
once we note that
\operatorname{Ext}^{\bullet}(\widehat{T}_{X}(6),{\mathcal{O}}(6))\simeq H^{\bullet}(X,\widehat{T}_{X}^{\vee})
. We can apply the same exact strategy to mutate
\widehat{T}_{X}(5)
one step to the right, obtaining:

\begin{split}\langle&{\mathcal{O}},{\mathcal{O}}(1),{\mathcal{O}}(2),{\mathcal{U}}^{\vee}(2),\Sym^{2}{\mathcal{U}}^{\vee}(2),{\mathcal{O}}(3),{\mathcal{U}}^{\vee}(3),\Sym^{2}{\mathcal{U}}^{\vee}(3),\\
&\hskip 40.0pt{\mathcal{O}}(4),{\mathcal{U}}^{\vee}(4),{\mathcal{O}}(5),{\mathcal{U}}(6),{\mathcal{U}}^{\vee}(5),{\mathcal{O}}(6),{\mathcal{U}}(7),{\mathcal{O}}(7)\rangle.\end{split}

Now, by Corollary
A.3
, the sequence
2.3
and Lemma
3.2
we easily see that
{\mathbb{L}}_{{\mathcal{O}}(k)}{\mathcal{U}}^{\vee}(k)\simeq{\mathcal{U}}(k)
for any
k
. Let us apply it to
k=2
in the collection above. Then, note that by Corollary
A.3
one has:

\operatorname{Ext}^{\bullet}({\mathcal{O}}(2),\Sym^{2}{\mathcal{U}}^{\vee}(2))={\mathbb{V}}^{D_{5}}_{2\omega_{1}}[0]

and therefore, by Lemma
3.2
one has
{\mathbb{L}}_{{\mathcal{O}}(2)}\Sym^{2}{\mathcal{U}}^{\vee}(2)=\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}(2)=\ker({\mathbb{V}}^{D_{5}}_{2\omega_{1}}\otimes{\mathcal{O}}(2)\longrightarrow\Sym^{2}{\mathcal{U}}^{\vee}(2))
(see the proof of Lemma
2.5
for more on this bundle). Now, we claim that
{\mathbb{L}}_{{\mathcal{U}}(2)}\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}(2)=\widehat{T}_{X}^{\vee}(1)
. In fact,
\operatorname{Ext}^{\bullet}({\mathcal{U}}(2),\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}(2))
can be computed by applying
\operatorname{Ext}({\mathcal{U}}(2),-)
to the sequence describing
\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}(2)
as a kernel (see Diagram
2.13
): in particular by Corollary
A.3
one has
\operatorname{Ext}^{\bullet}({\mathcal{U}}(2),{\mathcal{O}}(2))={\mathbb{V}}^{D_{5}}_{\omega_{1}}[0]
, and, together with Corollary
A.4
, this allows to conclude that
\operatorname{Ext}^{\bullet}({\mathcal{U}}(2),\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}(2))\simeq{\mathbb{V}}^{D_{5}}_{\omega_{1}}[0]
. From this and Lemma
3.2
the claim follows. Let us now mutate the resulting
\widehat{T}_{X}^{\vee}(1)
a further step to the left. We have
\operatorname{Ext}^{\bullet}({\mathcal{O}}(1),\widehat{T}_{X}^{\vee}(1))\simeq V_{\omega_{4}}[0]
, and therefore, by the appropriate twist of the dual of Equation
2.10
, we find
{\mathbb{L}}_{{\mathcal{O}}(1)}\widehat{T}_{X}^{\vee}(1)\simeq{\mathcal{U}}^{\vee}
.
If we repeat the same exact procedure on
\Sym^{2}{\mathcal{U}}^{\vee}(3)
we get the collection:

\begin{split}\langle&{\mathcal{O}},{\mathcal{U}}^{\vee},{\mathcal{O}}(1),{\mathcal{U}}^{\vee}(1),{\mathcal{O}}(2),{\mathcal{U}}^{\vee}(2),{\mathcal{O}}(3),{\mathcal{U}}^{\vee}(3),\\
&\hskip 40.0pt{\mathcal{O}}(4),{\mathcal{U}}^{\vee}(4),{\mathcal{O}}(5),{\mathcal{U}}(6),{\mathcal{U}}^{\vee}(5),{\mathcal{O}}(6),{\mathcal{U}}(7),{\mathcal{O}}(7)\rangle.\end{split}

Since by Lemma
A.8
we have
\operatorname{Ext}({\mathcal{U}}(6),{\mathcal{U}}^{\vee}(5))=0
, we can freely exchange such objects. The last step is to mutate each bundle of the form
{\mathcal{U}}(k)
one step to the right. One has
\operatorname{Ext}^{\bullet}({\mathcal{U}}(k),{\mathcal{O}}(k))={\mathbb{V}}^{D_{5}}_{\omega_{1}}[0]
, and by the sequence
2.3
and Lemma
3.2
we obtain
{\mathbb{R}}_{{\mathcal{O}}(k)}{\mathcal{U}}(k)\simeq{\mathcal{U}}^{\vee}(k)
. This leads to Kuznetsov’s collection, hence concluding our proof.
∎

The immediate consequence of Proposition
3.8
is the following:

Corollary 3.9
.

(Theorem
1.1
)

The exceptional collection of Equation
1.1
is full.

Appendix A
Borel–Bott–Weil computations

In this appendix, we will gather all cohomological computations done by means of the Borel–Bott–Weil theorem
[
Bot57
]
. This well-known theorem provides a simple, algorithmic recipe to compute the cohomology of irreducible vector bundles.

Theorem A.1

(Borel–Bott–Weil)
.

Let
{\mathcal{E}}_{\omega}
be a homogeneous, irreducible vector bundle on a rational homogeneous variety
G/P
, let us call
\rho
the sum of all fundamental weights. Then one and only one of the following statements is true:

(1)

there exists a sequence
s_{p}
of simple Weyl reflections of length
p
such that
s_{p}(\omega+\rho)-\rho
is dominant (i.e. all coefficients of its expansion in the fundamental weights are non-negative). Then
H^{p}(G/P,{\mathcal{E}}_{\omega})\simeq V^{G}_{s_{p}(\omega+\rho)-\rho}
and all the other cohomology is trivial.

(2)

there is no such
s_{p}
as in point (1). Then
{\mathcal{E}}_{\omega}
has no cohomology.

While we chose to illustrate all computations in detail, the algorithm can be easily automatized. See, for example, the script
[
Ram22
]
.

A.1.
Cohomology computations on
D_{5}/P^{4}

Here we follow the notation we fixed in Section
2.1.3
.

Lemma A.2
.

For every nonnegatgive
a,b,c,d\in{\mathbb{Z}}
, one has
H^{\bullet}(X,{\mathcal{E}}_{a\omega_{1}+b\omega_{2}+c\omega_{3}+d\omega_{5}})={\mathbb{V}}^{D_{5}}_{a\omega_{1}+b\omega_{2}+c\omega_{3}+d\omega_{5}}[0]
.

Proof.

The weight
a\omega_{1}+b\omega_{2}+c\omega_{3}+d\omega_{5}
is already dominant: by Theorem
A.1
we immediately conclude.
∎

Corollary A.3
.

For every nonnegative integer
r
, one has
H^{\bullet}(X,\Sym^{r}{\mathcal{U}}^{\vee})={\mathbb{V}}^{D_{5}}_{r\omega_{1}}[0]
.

Proof.

The weight associated to
\Sym^{r}{\mathcal{U}}^{\vee}
is
r\omega_{1}
(see Equation
2.2
), hence we conclude by Lemma
A.2
.
∎

Corollary A.4
.

There are the following isomorphisms:

\operatorname{Ext}^{\bullet}({\mathcal{U}}(2),\Sym^{2}{\mathcal{U}}^{\vee}(2))\simeq H^{\bullet}(X,\Sym^{3}{\mathcal{U}}^{\vee}\oplus{\mathcal{E}}_{\omega_{1}+\omega_{2}})\simeq{\mathbb{V}}^{D_{5}}_{3\omega_{1}}[0]\oplus{\mathbb{V}}^{D_{5}}_{\omega_{1}+\omega_{2}}[0].

Proof.

The first step is simply Lemma
2.1
, then we conclude by Corollary
A.3
.
∎

Lemma A.5
.

For all integers
a\geq 0
,
b\geq 0
,
c>0
and for
\epsilon\in\{1,2\}
one has:

\begin{split}H^{\bullet}(X,{\mathcal{E}}_{a\omega_{1}+b\omega_{2}-\epsilon\omega_{4}})&=0\\
H^{\bullet}(X,{\mathcal{E}}_{a\omega_{1}+b\omega_{2}+c\omega_{3}-2\omega_{4}})&={\mathbb{V}}^{D_{5}}_{a\omega_{1}+b\omega_{2}+(c-1)\omega_{3}}[-1].\end{split}

Proof.

An intuitive way to apply the Borel–Bott–Weil algorithm is to write the coefficients of the weight of the bundle as labels for the nodes of the Dynkin diagram, then add the sum
\rho
of the fundamental weights, and then to apply the Weyl reflections associated to simple roots. By adding
\rho
to the weight
a\omega_{1}+b\omega_{2}-\epsilon\omega_{4}
we find:

Observe that for
\epsilon=1
we have a zero coefficient: hence, the weight is fixed by the action of
S_{\alpha_{4}}
and there is no finite number of simple Weyl reflections which transforms it to a weight of the form
\rho+\lambda
with
\lambda
dominant. Thus, the associated bundle has no cohomology by Theorem
A.1
.
On the other hand, if
\epsilon=2
, one has

Note that if we subtract
\rho
to the resulting weight we obtain the dominant weight
a\omega_{1}+b\omega_{2}+(c-1)\omega_{3}
, and the result follows again by Theorem
A.1
.
∎

Corollary A.6
.

One has
H^{\bullet}(X,\widehat{T}_{X}^{\vee})\simeq{\mathbb{V}}^{D_{5}}_{\omega_{4}}[0]
.

Proof.

Consider the dual of the sequence
2.10
. The claim follows once we observe that by Lemma
A.5
the bundle
{\mathcal{U}}^{\vee}(-1)={\mathcal{E}}_{\omega_{1}-\omega_{4}}
has no cohomology.
∎

Lemma A.7
.

The vector bundle
{\mathcal{U}}
has no cohomology.

Proof.

Since
{\mathcal{U}}\simeq\wedge^{4}{\mathcal{U}}(-2)\simeq{\mathcal{E}}_{-\omega_{4}+\omega_{5}}
, the proof is immediate: one has

Lemma A.8
.

We have
\operatorname{Ext}^{\bullet}({\mathcal{U}}(1),{\mathcal{U}}^{\vee})=0
.

Proof.

We begin by decomposing the relevant bundle in a sum of irreducible, by Lemma
2.1
:

\begin{split}\operatorname{Ext}^{\bullet}({\mathcal{U}}(1),{\mathcal{U}}^{\vee})&=H^{\bullet}(X,{\mathcal{U}}^{\vee}\otimes{\mathcal{U}}^{\vee}(-1))\\
&=H^{\bullet}(X,\Sym^{2}{\mathcal{U}}^{\vee}(-1)\oplus\wedge^{2}{\mathcal{U}}^{\vee}(-1))\\
&=H^{\bullet}(X,{\mathcal{E}}_{2\omega_{1}-\omega_{4}}\oplus{\mathcal{E}}_{\omega_{2}-\omega_{4}}).\end{split}

Then, the proof follows by applying Lemma
A.5
.
∎

Lemma A.9
.

One has
\operatorname{Ext}(\Sym^{2}{\mathcal{U}}^{\vee}(2),{\mathcal{U}}^{\vee})={\mathbb{C}}[-3]
.

Proof.

One possibility is to expand the tensor product
\Sym^{2}{\mathcal{U}}\otimes{\mathcal{U}}^{\vee}(-2)
in irreducibles, and then apply again the Borel–Bott–Weil algorithm. However, note that by the appropriate Schur power of Equation
2.3
we can resolve
\Sym^{2}{\mathcal{U}}
by means of direct sums of wedge powers of
{\mathcal{U}}^{\vee}
, which are simpler objects. In fact, one has an exact sequence:

(A.1)

0\longrightarrow\Sym^{2}{\mathcal{U}}\otimes{\mathcal{U}}^{\vee}(-2)\longrightarrow\Sym^{2}{\mathbb{V}}^{D_{5}}_{\omega_{1}}\otimes{\mathcal{U}}^{\vee}(-2)\longrightarrow{\mathbb{V}}^{D_{5}}_{\omega_{1}}\otimes{\mathcal{U}}^{\vee}\otimes{\mathcal{U}}^{\vee}(-2)\longrightarrow\wedge^{2}{\mathcal{U}}^{\vee}\otimes{\mathcal{U}}^{\vee}(-2)\longrightarrow 0

The two middle terms have no cohomology: in fact, the first one is a direct sum of
55
copies of
{\mathcal{U}}^{\vee}(-2)\simeq{\mathcal{E}}_{\omega_{1}-2\omega_{4}}
, while the second one is a direct sum of
10
copies of
{\mathcal{U}}^{\vee}\otimes{\mathcal{U}}^{\vee}(-2)={\mathcal{E}}_{2\omega_{1}-2\omega_{4}}\oplus{\mathcal{E}}_{\omega_{2}-2\omega_{4}}
and all the irreducible direct summands have no cohomology by Lemma
A.5
. On the other hand, the last term decomposes as:

\wedge^{2}{\mathcal{U}}^{\vee}\otimes{\mathcal{U}}^{\vee}(-2)\simeq{\mathcal{E}}_{\omega_{1}+\omega_{2}-2\omega_{4}}\oplus{\mathcal{E}}_{\omega_{3}-2\omega_{4}}

and, while the first summand has no cohomology, the second contributes with
H^{1}(X,{\mathcal{E}}_{\omega_{3}-2\omega_{4}})\simeq{\mathbb{C}}
(again by Lemma
A.5
). In light of the sequence
A.1
, this completes the proof.
∎

Lemma A.10
.

We have
\operatorname{Ext}^{\bullet}(\Sym^{2}{\mathcal{U}}^{\vee},\widehat{T}^{\vee}(-1))={\mathbb{C}}[-2]
.

Proof.

This is equivalent to compute
H^{\bullet}(X,\Sym^{2}{\mathcal{U}}\otimes\widehat{T}^{\vee}(-1))
. By the dual of the sequence
2.10
we can easily resolve
\Sym^{2}{\mathcal{U}}\otimes\widehat{T}^{\vee}(-1)
as the kernel of the injection
\Sym^{2}{\mathcal{U}}\otimes{\mathcal{U}}^{\vee}(-2)\xhookrightarrow{\,\,\,\,\,}{\mathbb{V}}^{D_{5}}_{\omega_{4}}\otimes\Sym^{2}{\mathcal{U}}(-1)
. The first bundle has cohomology
{\mathbb{C}}[-3]
by Lemma
A.9
, while
\Sym^{2}{\mathcal{U}}(-1)
can be resolved by the sequence:

0\longrightarrow\Sym^{2}{\mathcal{U}}(-1)\longrightarrow\Sym^{2}{\mathbb{V}}^{D_{5}}_{\omega_{1}}\otimes{\mathcal{O}}(-1)\longrightarrow{\mathbb{V}}^{D_{5}}_{\omega_{1}}\otimes{\mathcal{U}}^{\vee}(-1)\longrightarrow\wedge^{2}{\mathcal{U}}^{\vee}(-1)\longrightarrow 0

and all bundles have no cohomology by Lemma
A.5
. This completes the proof.
∎

A.2.
Cohomology computations on
B_{4}/Q^{4}

The notation of this paragraph has been established in Section
2.1.4
.

Lemma A.11
.

One has:

\begin{split}H^{\bullet}(X,\wedge^{3}{\mathcal{R}}^{\vee}(-2))&\simeq{\mathbb{C}}[-1]\\
H^{\bullet}(X,{\mathcal{E}}_{\nu_{1}+\nu_{2}-2\nu_{4}})&=0\end{split}

Proof.

By Equation
2.4
we see that
\wedge^{3}{\mathcal{R}}^{\vee}(-2)={\mathcal{E}}_{\nu_{3}-2\nu_{4}}
. We proceed with the same algorithm as before but for
B_{4}
. By adding
\rho
we find:

{}{}\hbox to85.11pt{\vbox to12.72pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 4.08307pt\lower-2.56496pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \hbox to0.0pt{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox to0.0pt{{

\lxSVG@closescope \hss}{
{}{}{}}{}{}{{}}{}{
{}{}{}}{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setdash{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 34.59 2.98 L 69.19 2.98}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope \hss}{

{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}{

By Theorem
A.1
, we have cohomology if with a finite number of simple Weyl reflections we find a dominant weight: hence, we act with
S_{\beta_{4}}
in order to get rid of the negative coefficient. We find:

At this point, it is clear that if we subtract
\rho
from the resulting weight, we get a dominant weight (the trivial one). Hence we conclude that the cohomology of
{\mathcal{E}}_{\nu_{3}-2\nu_{4}}
is one dimensional in degree given by the number of simple Weyl reflections we used, which is one.

Let us consider the second claim. Here, applying
\rho
to the weight
\nu_{1}+\nu_{2}-2\nu_{4}
we find:

As above, we found a zero coefficient: Therefore, by Theorem
A.1
,
{\mathcal{E}}_{\nu_{1}+\nu_{2}-2\nu_{4}}
has no cohomology.
∎

Lemma A.12
.

We have
\operatorname{Ext}_{G}^{\bullet}(\wedge^{2}{\mathcal{R}}^{\vee},{\mathcal{R}}^{\vee})={\mathbb{C}}[-1]

Proof.

The proof follows immediately by the decomposition
\wedge^{2}{\mathcal{R}}\otimes{\mathcal{R}}^{\vee}\simeq\wedge^{2}{\mathcal{R}}^{\vee}\otimes\wedge^{2}{\mathcal{R}}^{\vee}(-2)\simeq{\mathcal{E}}_{\nu_{1}+\nu_{2}-2\nu_{5}}\oplus{\mathcal{E}}_{\nu_{3}-2\nu_{4}}
together with lemma
A.11
.
∎

Corollary A.13
.

One has
\operatorname{Ext}^{\bullet}({\mathcal{O}},{\mathcal{R}})={\mathbb{C}}[-1]
.

Proof.

Since
\operatorname{Ext}^{\bullet}({\mathcal{O}},{\mathcal{R}})\simeq H^{\bullet}(X,{\mathcal{R}})
, the proof follows simply by the isomorphism
{\mathcal{R}}\simeq\wedge^{3}{\mathcal{R}}^{\vee}(-2)
(Lemma
2.1
) and by Lemma
A.11
.
∎

Lemma A.14
.

The vanishing
H^{0}(X,\wedge^{r}{\mathcal{R}}(-c))=0
holds for any
c\geq-1
and
1\leq r\leq 3
.

Proof.

The proof follows the same identical reasoning of the one of Lemma
A.11
, once we write
\wedge^{r}{\mathcal{R}}(-c)={\mathcal{E}}_{\nu_{r}-c\nu_{4}}
.
∎

Lemma A.15
.

One has
H^{\bullet}(X,\Sym^{2}{\mathcal{R}})=0
.

Proof.

A quick way to prove this claim is to resolve
\Sym^{2}{\mathcal{R}}
in terms of
D_{5}
-homogeneous bundles and then apply some results of the previous subsection. By the appropriate Schur power of the sequence
2.6
we obtain:

(A.2)

0\longrightarrow\Sym^{2}{\mathcal{R}}\longrightarrow\Sym^{2}{\mathcal{U}}\longrightarrow{\mathcal{U}}\longrightarrow 0.

The last bundle has no cohomology by Lemma
A.7
, while the middle term can be resolved by a Schur power of the sequence
2.3
:

(A.3)

0\longrightarrow\Sym^{2}{\mathcal{U}}\longrightarrow{\mathbb{V}}^{D_{5}}_{\omega_{1}}\otimes{\mathcal{U}}\longrightarrow\wedge^{2}{\mathbb{V}}^{D_{5}}_{\omega_{1}}\otimes{\mathcal{O}}\longrightarrow\wedge^{2}{\mathcal{U}}^{\vee}\longrightarrow 0.

Note that the last arrow is an isomorphism at the level of cohomology (it is the evaluation map on sections and
\wedge^{2}{\mathcal{U}}^{\vee}
is globally generated). Then, we conclude by Lemma
A.7
again.
∎

Lemma A.16
.

We have
H^{\bullet}(X,\Sym^{2}{\mathcal{R}}\otimes{\mathcal{U}}^{\vee})^{G}={\mathbb{C}}[-1]
.

Proof.

By the tensor power of the sequence
A.2
with
{\mathcal{U}}^{\vee}
we find:

(A.4)

0\longrightarrow\Sym^{2}{\mathcal{R}}\otimes{\mathcal{U}}^{\vee}\longrightarrow\Sym^{2}{\mathcal{U}}\otimes{\mathcal{U}}^{\vee}\longrightarrow{\mathcal{U}}\otimes{\mathcal{U}}^{\vee}\longrightarrow 0.

The second term is resolved again by the tensor product of
{\mathcal{U}}^{\vee}
with a Schur power of the sequence
A.3
:

0\longrightarrow\Sym^{2}{\mathcal{U}}\otimes{\mathcal{U}}^{\vee}\longrightarrow\Sym^{2}{\mathbb{V}}^{D_{5}}_{\omega_{1}}\otimes{\mathcal{U}}^{\vee}\longrightarrow{\mathbb{V}}^{D_{5}}_{\omega_{1}}\otimes{\mathcal{U}}^{\vee}\otimes{\mathcal{U}}^{\vee}\longrightarrow\wedge^{2}{\mathcal{U}}^{\vee}\otimes{\mathcal{U}}^{\vee}\longrightarrow 0.

In this last sequence, the cohomology of all terms but the first can be computed by the Littlewood–Richardson formula together with Lemma
A.2
. We find
H^{\bullet}(X,\Sym^{2}{\mathcal{U}}\otimes{\mathcal{U}}^{\vee})\simeq{\mathbb{V}}^{D_{5}}_{\omega_{1}}[-1]
. On the other hand, the last term of the sequence
A.4
contributes with
{\mathbb{C}}[-1]
(
{\mathcal{U}}
is an exceptional vector bundle in
D^{b}(X)
). We conclude by taking the space of invariants.
∎
